# Unit 17 linguistic and owned-edition mapping packet

This packet prepares review. It records an unsigned structural mapping proposal. It does not record a qualified review, an approved edition alignment, or an install decision.

The owned-edition mapping worksheet separates observed edition facts, the proposed lesson and printed page range, unresolved edition questions, and matters that still require qualified linguistic review. ISBN, owned-copy verification, variance, reviewer identity, qualification, date, and approval stay blank. The disposition remains PENDING_CURRICULUM_REVIEWER.

Unit 17 remains in development. Its readiness is content-complete and review-pending. The Lesson 17 relationship is a proposed, unsigned mapping. It is not an approved edition alignment.

It covers this unit and the reachable instructional records, relationships, shared dependencies, sources, rights, assets, and advancement rule. It does not cover any other unit. Recording a review status, reviewer signature, mapping approval, install state, or readiness does not change it.

`mappingPackageDigest` binds the proposed edition-mapping facts for Unit 17: edition identity, lesson, printed page range, topic description, observation source, ISBN observation state, and the pending disposition. Reviewer identity, qualification, date, notes, correction notes, and signature stay outside that digest.

`packContentReviewDigest` is the repository aggregate of instructional curriculum. It is not an approval of this unit, and it is not part of the review-package digest.

`reviewPackageDigest` identifies this complete pending review package. It binds the ordered instructional records and the mapping-package digest. Changing a proposed page range or topic changes the mapping-package digest and therefore this package digest.

`repositorySourceDigest` binds repository and release inputs. Recording an approval later changes that repository hash. That change does not replace the signed content-review digest or the mapping-package digest.

The marker scan used by this package detects only a fixed list of known prohibited mapping-input markers. It is not comprehensive copyright detection and cannot recognize every copy or close paraphrase of protected material.

- Content hash: a00df45475e57ca12eebf75ee09393213ea74d282ddd5b68ede1e5ef0d5f4895

kind=TEACH
title=Admitted instruction
body=In Russia, dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "28.08.17") and the expanded form (for example "28 августа 2017 г."); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (goda, "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "28.08.2017") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral grammatical gender), then the month in genitive case (for example "Двадцать восьмое августа").
dominantAction=Continue
references=ref-u1620-attribution
items=
examples=
unavailableReason=
bookDirection=
companionDirection=
remediationStepId=

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Date_and_time_notation_in_Russia&oldid=1357134172
sourceRevision=1357134172
sourceRecordId=date-russia:In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This o
originalSourceText=In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.17}}") and the expanded form (for example "{{lang|ru|28 августа 2017 г.}}"); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (''goda'', "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.2017}}") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral [[grammatical gender]]), then the month in [[genitive case]] (for example "{{lang|ru|Двадцать восьмое августа}}").
shippedText=the month in genitive case

originalSourceText=In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.17}}") and the expanded form (for example "{{lang|ru|28 августа 2017 г.}}"); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (''goda'', "of the year"). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.2017}}") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral [[grammatical gender]]), then the month in [[genitive case]] (for example "{{lang|ru|Двадцать восьмое августа}}").

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Date_and_time_notation_in_Russia&oldid=1357134172
sourceRevision=1357134172
sourceRecordId=date-russia:In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This o
originalSourceText=In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.17}}") and the expanded form (for example "{{lang|ru|28 августа 2017 г.}}"); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (''goda'', "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.2017}}") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral [[grammatical gender]]), then the month in [[genitive case]] (for example "{{lang|ru|Двадцать восьмое августа}}").
shippedText=day month year
transformation=selection

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Numbers?oldid=4371358
sourceRevision=4371358
sourceRecordId=numbers:==Ordinal numbers==

Ordinal numerals in Russian are relatively easy to deal wit
originalSourceText===Ordinal numbers==

Ordinal numerals in Russian are relatively easy to deal with.  They all decline like adjectives.  For example: Только что фотографировала восемнадцатую девушку (She just photographed the eighteenth girl).

# п'''е'''рвый, п'''е'''рвая, п'''е'''рвое
# втор'''о'''й, втор'''а'''я, втор'''о'''е
# тр'''е'''тий, тр'''е'''тья, тр'''е'''тье
# четв'''ё'''ртый

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Numbers?oldid=4371358
sourceRevision=4371358
sourceRecordId=numbers:==Ordinal numbers==

Ordinal numerals in Russian are relatively easy to deal wit
originalSourceText===Ordinal numbers==

Ordinal numerals in Russian are relatively easy to deal with.  They all decline like adjectives.  For example: Только что фотографировала восемнадцатую девушку (She just photographed the eighteenth girl).

# п'''е'''рвый, п'''е'''рвая, п'''е'''рвое
# втор'''о'''й, втор'''а'''я, втор'''о'''е
# тр'''е'''тий, тр'''е'''тья, тр'''е'''тье
# четв'''ё'''ртый

- Content hash: 65d15cded4662d191de651e5f05937c1383b4ca6eed2141e920343cf3ec7beee

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Vocabulary/Days_of_the_Week?oldid=3594124
sourceRevision=3594124
sourceRecordId=days:Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyeh
originalSourceText=Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyehn
|-
| colspan="4" | A cognate (same word in Russian and English).
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| сегодня || noun || today || sevódnya
|-
| colspan="4" | The г here is pronounced as a в. It sounds like "с его дня", which would mean "from his day", if день were an animate noun. But it's not.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| понедельник || noun || Monday || ponyedyél'nik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Mondays we ride our ''ponies'' to ''Nick's deli''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| вторник || noun || Tuesday || vtórnik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Tuesdays we go to the ''store'' with ''Nick''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| среда || noun || Wednesday || sryéda
|-
| colspan="4" | Wednesdays are a good day for ''shredding''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| четверг || noun || Thursday || chetvérg
|-
| colspan="4" | On Thursdays ''Chet'' goes to ''work''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| пятница || noun || Friday || pyátnitsa
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Fridays we eat ''pizza'' on a ''yacht'', ''neatly''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| суббота || noun || Saturday || soobbóta
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Saturdays ''Sue'' goes ''boating''. Also Saturday is the ''Sabbath''. Don't confuse суббота with собакa, that is a dog.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| воскресенье || noun || Sunday
shippedText=Monday
transformation=selection
licenseId=CC BY-SA 4.0
licenseUrl=https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/
attribution=Wikibooks contributors, CC BY-SA 4.0, via Wikibooks:Copyrights oldid 4622060. Page history for the frozen oldid identifies the contributors.

- Content hash: b488fa6944172deb129f6a3800bde43f9b99972c76856af33139693b6242546c

recordType=meaning
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Vocabulary/Days_of_the_Week?oldid=3594124
sourceRevision=3594124
sourceRecordId=days:Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyeh
originalSourceText=Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyehn
|-
| colspan="4" | A cognate (same word in Russian and English).
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| сегодня || noun || today || sevódnya
|-
| colspan="4" | The г here is pronounced as a в. It sounds like "с его дня", which would mean "from his day", if день were an animate noun. But it's not.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| понедельник || noun || Monday || ponyedyél'nik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Mondays we ride our ''ponies'' to ''Nick's deli''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| вторник || noun || Tuesday || vtórnik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Tuesdays we go to the ''store'' with ''Nick''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| среда || noun || Wednesday || sryéda
|-
| colspan="4" | Wednesdays are a good day for ''shredding''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| четверг || noun || Thursday || chetvérg
|-
| colspan="4" | On Thursdays ''Chet'' goes to ''work''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| пятница || noun || Friday || pyátnitsa
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Fridays we eat ''pizza'' on a ''yacht'', ''neatly''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| суббота || noun || Saturday || soobbóta
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Saturdays ''Sue'' goes ''boating''. Also Saturday is the ''Sabbath''. Don't confuse суббота with собакa, that is a dog.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| воскресенье || noun || Sunday

recordType=instruction
sourceUrl=https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Date_and_time_notation_in_Russia&oldid=1357134172
sourceRevision=1357134172
sourceRecordId=date-russia:In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This o
originalSourceText=In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.17}}") and the expanded form (for example "{{lang|ru|28 августа 2017 г.}}"); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (''goda'', "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.2017}}") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral [[grammatical gender]]), then the month in [[genitive case]] (for example "{{lang|ru|Двадцать восьмое августа}}").
shippedText=In Russia, dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "28.08.17") and the expanded form (for example "28 августа 2017 г."); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (goda, "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "28.08.2017") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral grammatical gender), then the month in genitive case (for example "Двадцать восьмое августа").
transformation=wiki label selection
licenseId=CC BY-SA 4.0
licenseUrl=https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/
attribution=Wikipedia contributors, CC BY-SA 4.0, via Wikipedia:Copyrights oldid 1369466282. Page history for oldid 1357134172 identifies the contributors.
shareAlikeTreatment=Adapted curriculum data, selection, and arrangement are distributed under CC BY-SA 4.0. Application code, user data, and user recordings are outside that statement. No audio is relicensed because this unit ships no audio.

recordType=label
sourceUrl=https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Date_and_time_notation_in_Russia&oldid=1357134172
sourceRevision=1357134172
sourceRecordId=date-russia:In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This o
originalSourceText=In [[Russia]], dates are usually written in "day month year" (DMY) order. This order is used in both the all-numeric date (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.17}}") and the expanded form (for example "{{lang|ru|28 августа 2017 г.}}"); the trailing "г" is short for "года" (''goda'', "of the year"). Coincidentally, in Polish the word for year is "rok", so a similar date format is used by the Poles e.g. 1987r). Single-digit numbers for day or month may have a preceding zero (for example "{{lang|ru|28.08.2017}}") is more usual.

When saying the date, it is usually pronounced using the ordinal number of the day first (in neutral [[grammatical gender]]), then the month in [[genitive case]] (for example "{{lang|ru|Двадцать восьмое августа}}").
shippedText=the ordinal number of the day first

recordType=instruction
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Numbers?oldid=4371358
sourceRevision=4371358
sourceRecordId=numbers:==Ordinal numbers==

Ordinal numerals in Russian are relatively easy to deal wit
originalSourceText===Ordinal numbers==

Ordinal numerals in Russian are relatively easy to deal with.  They all decline like adjectives.  For example: Только что фотографировала восемнадцатую девушку (She just photographed the eighteenth girl).

# п'''е'''рвый, п'''е'''рвая, п'''е'''рвое
# втор'''о'''й, втор'''а'''я, втор'''о'''е
# тр'''е'''тий, тр'''е'''тья, тр'''е'''тье
# четв'''ё'''ртый

recordType=instruction
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Vocabulary/Days_of_the_Week?oldid=3594124
sourceRevision=3594124
sourceRecordId=days:Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyeh
originalSourceText=Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyehn
|-
| colspan="4" | A cognate (same word in Russian and English).
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| сегодня || noun || today || sevódnya
|-
| colspan="4" | The г here is pronounced as a в. It sounds like "с его дня", which would mean "from his day", if день were an animate noun. But it's not.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| понедельник || noun || Monday || ponyedyél'nik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Mondays we ride our ''ponies'' to ''Nick's deli''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| вторник || noun || Tuesday || vtórnik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Tuesdays we go to the ''store'' with ''Nick''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| среда || noun || Wednesday || sryéda
|-
| colspan="4" | Wednesdays are a good day for ''shredding''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| четверг || noun || Thursday || chetvérg
|-
| colspan="4" | On Thursdays ''Chet'' goes to ''work''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| пятница || noun || Friday || pyátnitsa
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Fridays we eat ''pizza'' on a ''yacht'', ''neatly''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| суббота || noun || Saturday || soobbóta
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Saturdays ''Sue'' goes ''boating''. Also Saturday is the ''Sabbath''. Don't confuse суббота with собакa, that is a dog.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| воскресенье || noun || Sunday
shippedText=Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

sourceRecordId=days:Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyeh
originalSourceText=Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyehn
|-
| colspan="4" | A cognate (same word in Russian and English).
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| сегодня || noun || today || sevódnya
|-
| colspan="4" | The г here is pronounced as a в. It sounds like "с его дня", which would mean "from his day", if день were an animate noun. But it's not.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| понедельник || noun || Monday || ponyedyél'nik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Mondays we ride our ''ponies'' to ''Nick's deli''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| вторник || noun || Tuesday || vtórnik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Tuesdays we go to the ''store'' with ''Nick''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| среда || noun || Wednesday || sryéda
|-
| colspan="4" | Wednesdays are a good day for ''shredding''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| четверг || noun || Thursday || chetvérg
|-
| colspan="4" | On Thursdays ''Chet'' goes to ''work''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| пятница || noun || Friday || pyátnitsa
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Fridays we eat ''pizza'' on a ''yacht'', ''neatly''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| суббота || noun || Saturday || soobbóta
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Saturdays ''Sue'' goes ''boating''. Also Saturday is the ''Sabbath''. Don't confuse суббота with собакa, that is a dog.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| воскресенье || noun || Sunday

recordType=label
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Vocabulary/Days_of_the_Week?oldid=3594124
sourceRevision=3594124
sourceRecordId=days:Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyeh
originalSourceText=Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyehn
|-
| colspan="4" | A cognate (same word in Russian and English).
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| сегодня || noun || today || sevódnya
|-
| colspan="4" | The г here is pronounced as a в. It sounds like "с его дня", which would mean "from his day", if день were an animate noun. But it's not.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| понедельник || noun || Monday || ponyedyél'nik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Mondays we ride our ''ponies'' to ''Nick's deli''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| вторник || noun || Tuesday || vtórnik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Tuesdays we go to the ''store'' with ''Nick''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| среда || noun || Wednesday || sryéda
|-
| colspan="4" | Wednesdays are a good day for ''shredding''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| четверг || noun || Thursday || chetvérg
|-
| colspan="4" | On Thursdays ''Chet'' goes to ''work''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| пятница || noun || Friday || pyátnitsa
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Fridays we eat ''pizza'' on a ''yacht'', ''neatly''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| суббота || noun || Saturday || soobbóta
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Saturdays ''Sue'' goes ''boating''. Also Saturday is the ''Sabbath''. Don't confuse суббота with собакa, that is a dog.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| воскресенье || noun || Sunday
shippedText=Saturday

recordType=label
sourceUrl=https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Russian/Vocabulary/Days_of_the_Week?oldid=3594124
sourceRevision=3594124
sourceRecordId=days:Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyeh
originalSourceText=Russians don't capitalize days of the week.

{|
|-
| день || noun || day || dyehn
|-
| colspan="4" | A cognate (same word in Russian and English).
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| сегодня || noun || today || sevódnya
|-
| colspan="4" | The г here is pronounced as a в. It sounds like "с его дня", which would mean "from his day", if день were an animate noun. But it's not.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| понедельник || noun || Monday || ponyedyél'nik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Mondays we ride our ''ponies'' to ''Nick's deli''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| вторник || noun || Tuesday || vtórnik
|-
| colspan="4" | On Tuesdays we go to the ''store'' with ''Nick''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| среда || noun || Wednesday || sryéda
|-
| colspan="4" | Wednesdays are a good day for ''shredding''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| четверг || noun || Thursday || chetvérg
|-
| colspan="4" | On Thursdays ''Chet'' goes to ''work''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| пятница || noun || Friday || pyátnitsa
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Fridays we eat ''pizza'' on a ''yacht'', ''neatly''.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| суббота || noun || Saturday || soobbóta
|-
| colspan="4" | "On Saturdays ''Sue'' goes ''boating''. Also Saturday is the ''Sabbath''. Don't confuse суббота with собакa, that is a dog.
|-
| &nbsp;
|-
| воскресенье || noun || Sunday
